Construction of two-bubble blow-up solutions for the mass-critical gKdV equations Thanks: Y. L. was partially funded by the New Cornerstone Investigator Program 100001127.
Yang Lan, Xu Yuan

Abstract
. For the mass-critical generalized Korteweg-de Vries equation, ∂ t u + ∂ x ( ∂ x 2 u + u 5 ) = 0 , ( t , x ) ∈ [ 0 , ∞ ) × ℝ , \partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}\left(\partial_{x}^{2}u+u^{5}\right)=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}, We prove the existence of a global solution that blows up in infinite time and approaches the sum of two decoupled bubbles with opposite signs. The proof is inspired by the techniques developed for the two-dimensional mass-critical NLS equation in a similar context by Martel-Raphaël [ 37 ] . The main difficulty originates from the fact that the unstable directions related to scaling are excited by the nonlinear interactions. To overcome this difficulty, a refined approximate solution that involves some non-localized profiles is needed. In particular, a sharp understanding for the interactions between solitons and such profiles is also required.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35B40 (primary), 35Q51, 37Q53

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main result

In this article, we consider the dynamics of two-bubbles for the mass-critical generalized Korteweg-de Vries (gKdV) equation,

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}\left(\partial_{x}^{2}u+u^{5}\right)=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}.

(1.1)

The mass
M(u)
and energy
E(u)
are formally conserved by the flow of (
1.1
) where

M(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}{\rm d}x\quad\mbox{and}\quad E(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{x}u)^{2}-\frac{1}{6}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{6}{\rm d}x.

Recall that, the Cauchy problem for equation (
1.1
) is locally well-posed in the energy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
(see Kenig-Ponce-Vega
[
17
,
18
]
). More precisely, for any initial data
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, there exists a unique (in a certain class) maximal solution
u
of (
1.1
) in

C\left([0,T);H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\right)
. For this Cauchy problem, the following blow-up criterion holds:

T<\infty\Longrightarrow\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}=\infty\quad\mbox{as}\ t\uparrow T.

(1.2)

For such
H^{1}
solution
u
, the mass
M(u)
and energy
E(u)
are conserved on
[0,T)
.

Recall also that, if
u
is a solution of (
1.1
) then for any
\lambda>0
, the function

u_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}u\left(\lambda^{3}t,\lambda x\right),\quad\mbox{for}\ (t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

is also a solution of (
1.1
). In addition, this scaling symmetry keeps the
L^{2}
-norm invariant so that the problem is
mass-critical
.

We recall the family of solitary wave solutions of (
1.1
).
Let
Q(x)=\left(3{\mathrm{sech}}^{2}(2x)\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}
be the unique (up to translation) positive
H^{1}
solution of the equation

-Q^{\prime\prime}+Q-Q^{5}=0,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Then, for any
(\lambda_{0},x_{0})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, the function

u(t,x)=\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}Q\left(\lambda_{0}\left(x-\lambda_{0}^{2}t-x_{0}\right)\right),

is a solution of (
1.1
) called
solitary wave
or
soliton
. Following a variational argument
[
47
]
, the conservation laws and the blow-up criterion (
1.2
) imply that any initial data
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with subcritical mass,
i.e.
satisfying
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}<\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
, generates a
global and bounded
solution in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

To go beyond the threshold mass
\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
, it is natural to restrict to solutions with small supercritical mass,
i.e.
satisfying

\|Q\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}<(1+\delta)\|Q\|_{L^{2}},\quad\mbox{for}\ \ 0<\delta\ll 1.

(1.3)

The study of singularity formation for such case was first developed in a series of works by Martel-Merle
[
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
]
and Merle
[
39
]
via rigidity Liouville-type property, monotonicity formula and localized Virial estimate. Later on, Martel-Merle-Raphaël
[
33
,
34
,
35
]
revisited the blow-up analysis for (
1.1
), giving a comprehensive description of the flow near the soliton and thereby completing the results in the above-mentioned works. See Section
1.2
for more discussion.

For the multi-bubble dynamics of (
1.1
), it is known from Combet-Martel
[
5
]
that
finite time
blow-up solutions exist with an arbitrary number of bubbles and any choice of signs.
In this article, we construct the first example of
infinite time
blow-up solution of (
1.1
) related to the strong interactions of two bubbles with opposite signs. Our main result is formulated as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a global-in-time solution
u\in C\left([0,\infty);H^{1}\right)
of (
1.1
) that decomposes asymptotically into a sum of two bubbles at infinity:

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left\|u(t)-\frac{1}{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)}\sum_{n=1,2}(-1)^{n+1}Q\left(\frac{\cdot-x_{n}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}=0.

Here, the position parameters
(x_{1},x_{2})
satisfy

x_{2}(t)-x_{1}(t)=(2+o(1))\log^{\frac{5}{6}}t,\quad\mbox{as}\ t\uparrow\infty.

In addition, the scaling and position parameters
(\lambda(t),x_{1}(t))
satisfy

\displaystyle\left(\lambda(t),x_{1}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1+o(1)\right)\left(\frac{1}{\log^{\frac{1}{6}}t},t\log^{\frac{1}{3}}t\right),\ \mbox{as}\ t\uparrow\infty.

In particular, the blow-up rate for
u(t)
is

\|\partial_{x}u(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=(2+o(1))\|Q^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\log^{\frac{1}{3}}t,\quad\mbox{as}\ \ t\uparrow\infty.

Remark 1.2
.

We mention here that, Theorem
1.1
deal with
strong interactions
in the sense that the blow-up dynamics of each bubble is perturbed at the main order by the presence of the another bubble (see Section
1.4
for more discussion).

Remark 1.3
.

The choice of signs and the number of bubbles are related to the solvability of a linear differential system. The source term of this system originates from the interaction between solitons and certain non-localized profiles (see Lemma
2.7
). Indeed, the system for two bubbles with the same sign is not solvable, and the multi-bubble case is far more involved than the two bubble case. This is the main reason why we restrict ourselves to the current case.

1.2.
Blow-up description and classification for gKdV equation

Let us first consider the set of initial data

\mathcal{A}=\left\{u_{0}=Q+\varepsilon_{0}:\|\varepsilon_{0}\|_{H^{1}}<\alpha_{0}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \int_{0}^{\infty}y^{10}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}{\rm d}y<1\right\},\ \ \mbox{with}\ \ 0<\alpha_{0}\ll 1.

For initial data in
H^{1}
close to the soliton with a suitable space decay property,
Martel-Merle-Raphaël
[
33
]
fully described
the blow-up dynamics
of the corresponding solution. More precisely, for initial data
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
, Martel-Merle-Raphaël
[
33
]
proved that only three possible behaviors can occur:

Exit:

The solution eventually exits any small neighborhood of the solitons.

Blow-up:

The solution blows up stably in finite time with rate
(T-t)^{-1}
.

Soliton:

: The solution is global and locally converges to a soliton.

Later on, building on the work
[
33
]
, Martel-Merle-Nakanishi-Raphaël
[
32
]
showed that there exists a local
C^{1}
co-dimension one manifold included in
\mathcal{A}
which separates the stable
blow-up behavior from solutions that eventually exit the soliton neighborhood.
In particular, the solutions on the manifold are
global in time
and converge in a local norm to a soliton.

On the other hand, Martel-Merle-Raphaël
[
34
]
established the
existence and description
of the minimal mass blow-up solution, a key step for the complete description of the flow around the soliton. Then, the sharp asymptotics in both time and space variables were derived by Combet-Martel
[
4
]
for any order derivative of this solution. Building on these sharp properties and the techniques developed mainly in
[
33
,
34
,
35
]
, Combet-Martel
[
5
]
constructed
finite time
multi-bubble blow-up solutions with an arbitrary number of bubbles. Such solutions are also related to the
strong interaction
of bubbles as can be seen from the presence of the tail to the left of the minimal mass blow-up solution (see
[
5
, Section 1.3]
for more discussion).

Since the assumption
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
, the above-mentioned stable blow-up result does not describe all blow-up solutions with the small supercritical mass (
1.3
). In Martel-Merle-Raphaël
[
35
]
, the authors showed that there exists a large class of
exotic
finite time blow-up solutions, close to the solitons, enjoying blow-up rates of the form
\|u(t)\|_{H^{1}}\sim(T-t)^{-\nu}
for any
\nu>\frac{11}{13}
. Global solutions
blowing up in
infinite time
with
\|u(t)\|_{H^{1}}\sim e^{t}
or
\|u(t)\|_{H^{1}}\sim t^{\nu}
for any
\nu>0
were also constructed in
[
35
]
. More recently, Manatova
[
24
]
extended these results, proving the existence of exotic blow-up solutions for any rate

(T-t)^{-\nu}
with
\nu\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right)
. We mention here that, the exponent
\nu=\frac{1}{2}
is critical: for
\nu\geq\frac{1}{2}
, the position of blow-up soliton goes to
\infty
, whereas for
\nu<\frac{1}{2}
, such position converges to a finite point. Recently, Martel-Pilod
[
26
]

constructed the first example of a finite-energy solution that blows up at a
finite spatial point
with the rate
(T-t)^{-\frac{2}{5}}
. Then, the same authors in
[
27
]
introduced a more general framework for the finite point blowup and proved the existence for such solutions for any blow-up rate
(T-t)^{-\nu}
with
\nu\in\left(\frac{3}{7},\frac{1}{2}\right)
.

The asymptotic behavior of solutions close to the soliton was also studied for
saturated nonlinearities
of the form
u^{5}-\gamma|u|^{p-1}u
for fixed
p>5
in the limit
\gamma\downarrow 0
in
[
21
]
. In particular,
flattening solitary waves
were constructed in
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
. Then, a full family of such flattening waves was constructed in Martel-Pilod
[
25
]
for (
1.1
). Last, we refer to
[
2
,
7
,
8
,
12
,
23
]
for some recent advances of the study for blow-up dynamics of 2D cubic Zakharov-Kutnetzov equation, which is a natural analogue of the mass-critical gKdV equation in two dimensions.

1.3.
Previous results for other models

In the last twenty years, there have been significant progress in the study of the blow-up dynamics for the mass-critical NLS equation. The first example of multi-bubble blow-up solution was constructed in Merle
[
38
]
. In particular, such solution blows up with the pseudo-conformal rate
\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sim t^{-1}
as
t\downarrow 0
. Recently, the uniqueness for such blow-up solutions was obtained in
[
3
]
. Some other multi-bubble type blow-up solutions for NLS equation were constructed by
[
11
,
46
]
in the context of the
log-log
regime.
We mention here that, these works deal with
weak interactions
in the sense that the leading order term of the soliton dynamics is not generated by the interactions.
Last, we refer to
[
1
,
20
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
45
]
and references therein for the study of single bubble blow-up dynamics of the mass-critical NLS equation.

For the NLS equation, the first multi-bubble blow-up solution with
strong interactions
was constructed by Martel-Raphaël
[
37
]
. In particular, they built an infinite-time blow-up solution involving the strong interaction of an arbitrary number
K\geq 2
of bubbles. Through the pseudo-conformal transform, this also yielded the first example of a
finite-time
blow-up solution with a rate strictly faster than the conformal one. Such a solution concentrates the
K
bubbles at one point at the blow-up time.

Following the spirit of
[
37
]
, we study in this article the
two-bubble dynamics
with strong interactions for the gKdV equation (
1.1
). However, at least two main difficulties arise, originating from structural differences between the two equations. The first difficulty originates from the linear growth of the blow-up profile on one side of
\mathbb{R}
. This requires us to carefully trace both
soliton?soliton
and
profile?soliton
interactions, rather than only those of the
soliton?soliton
type. A second, more significant difficulty arises from the presence of the parameter
\mu
, which relates to the difference between the scaling parameters of the two bubbles. Unlike in the NLS case, this extra parameter appears as a leading-order term in the ODE governing the distance parameter
z
. This requires us to determine the asymptotic behavior of this parameter, and thus, we must first obtain refined control for the parameters
(b_{1},b_{2})
which are related to the blow-up profile. See more discussion in Section
1.4
. We refer to
[
6
,
9
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
for some
related results of multi-bubble solutions with strong interactions for other critical models. We also refer to
[
10
,
13
,
19
,
22
,
36
,
43
,
44
]
for some related results of multi-soliton and multi-kink solutions in this regime for other dispersive models.

1.4.
A Roadmap of the proof

The blow-up solutions in Theorem
1.1
are obtained
using the interaction between two solitons.
More precisely, We first consider a time dependent
C^{1}
geometric function
\mathcal{G}
of the following form:

\mathcal{G}=(\lambda,z,\mu,x_{1},b_{1},b_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{4},

with
\lambda+|\mu|+|b_{1}|+|b_{2}|+z^{-1}\ll 1
.
Here, the function
\lambda
is the scaling parameter, the function
z
is the distance between the two bubbles and the function
\mu
is the difference of the scaling.
As usual in investigating the blow-up phenomenon for the mass-critical problem, we
introduce the following new space-time variables:

w(s,y)=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}(s)u(t(s),\lambda(s)y+x_{1}(s))\ \ \mbox{with}\ \ \frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}t}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}(t)}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ y=\frac{x-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)}.

Here,
u(t,x)
is a solution of (
1.1
). By an elementary computation, we find

\partial_{s}w+\partial_{y}\left(\partial_{y}^{2}w-w+w^{5}\right)-\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda}\Lambda w-\left(\frac{\dot{x}_{1}}{\lambda}-1\right)\partial_{y}w=0.

(1.4)

An approximate solution
V
of (
1.4
) is defined of the form

V=Q_{1}-Q_{2}+b_{1}X_{1}\phi-b_{2}X_{2}\phi+e^{-z}\left(A_{1}+B_{2}\right)\phi+\rm{L.O.T}.

Here,
(Q_{1},Q_{2})
are two soliton at different positions and
(X_{1},X_{2},A_{1},B_{2})
are suitable non-localized-profiles.
Note that, the leading-order terms of soliton-soliton interactions are generated by
e^{-z}(e^{\pm y}Q^{4})
. This is the main reason why we introduce the auxiliary functions
A_{1}
and
B_{2}
to construct the approximate solution. Due to the cancellation
(\Lambda Q,Q)=0
, the auxiliary functions
A_{1}
and
B_{2}
should exhibit a linear growth on the right-hand side of
\mathbb{R}
, which is essentially different from that in
[
37
,
43
]
. This difference leads to much more complicated interactions and ODE system related to parameters (see Lemma
2.12
and Section
2.3
for more details).

Due to the presence of the parameter
\mu
, the geometric parameters satisfy a different ODE system from that of the NLS equation (see
[
37
, Lemma 3]
).
More precisely, the parameters
(z,\mu,b_{1},b_{2})
satisfy

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{z}-b_{1}z-\mu&=\dot{b}_{1}+\alpha e^{-z}+2b_{1}^{2}={\rm{L.O.T}},\\
\dot{\mu}-2(b_{2}-b_{1})&=\dot{b}_{2}+\alpha e^{-z}+2b_{1}^{2}={\rm{L.O.T}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.5)

In order to treat the above system, we first investigate the asymptotic behavior of
\Theta=(b_{2}-b_{1})
. This step requires a refined estimate for the parameters
(b_{1},b_{2})
(see Lemma
3.9
).
Together with an analogous refinement for
\mu
(see Lemma
3.7
), we deduce that the size of
\mu
is the same as
b_{1}z
. Therefore, the ODE system (
1.5
) admits a different structure, and thus, it exhibits a behavior markedly different from that of the case of NLS equation. This is the main reason why the blow-up rate in Theorem
1.1
is different from the rate for the case of the NLS equation.

On the other hand, refined control of

\Theta
requires careful tracking of both soliton?soliton and soliton?profile interactions. For instance, while the leading-order soliton?soliton interaction is of
e^{-z}
, the next order
\mu ze^{-z}
also enters the refined estimate for
\Theta
(see Lemma
2.11
). Moreover, due to the linear growth of
A_{1}
and
B_{2}
, there are some terms with the same size appear in the soliton-profile type interaction (see Lemma
2.19
). Indeed, it turns out that at the leading order, the following equation of
(\mu,\Theta)
is verified

\dot{\mu}-2\Theta=\dot{\Theta}+\frac{5}{2}\alpha\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}(ze^{-z})=\rm{L.O.T}.

(1.6)

The ODE system (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) admits the following approximate solution:

\left\{\begin{aligned} z_{\rm{app}}(s)\sim 2\log s,\ \ b_{1{\rm{app}}}(s)\sim\frac{1}{6s\log s},\\
\mu_{\rm{app}}(s)\sim\frac{5}{3s}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Theta_{\rm{app}}(s)\sim-\frac{5}{6s^{2}},\end{aligned}\right.\Longrightarrow\lambda_{\rm{app}}(s)\sim\frac{1}{\log^{\frac{1}{6}}s}.

The above estimates mean that the blow-up rate in Theorem
1.1
is a reasonable guess for the first order asymptotics of some particular solutions of (
1.1
).

Last, we use a variant of an energy functional on
\varepsilon
to control it in the soliton region. The key point here is to estimate the error term
\varepsilon
backwards in time. From (
1.5
), a delicate scaling term related to
\mu
does not have a favorable sign. To overcome such difficulty, we employ the idea introduced in
[
43
, Section 3]
to construct a suitable localized refined term for the standard energy functional of
\varepsilon
. Based on such control and weak
H^{1}
stability of gKdV equation (
1.1
). we argue by compactness and obtain the infinite time blow-up solution
u
as the weak limit of a sequence of solutions
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

1.5.
Notation and conventions

We denote by
\mathcal{Y}
the set of smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
(c_{k},C_{k})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
such that

\big|f^{(k)}(y)\big|\leq C_{k}\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

We also denote

\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}):\mbox{there exists}\ g\in\mathcal{Y}\ \mbox{such that}\ f(y)=\int_{y}^{\infty}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right\}.

In addition, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}):\mbox{there exists}\ g\in\mathcal{Y}\ \mbox{such that}\ f(y)-f(0)=\int_{0}^{y}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{2}=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}):\mbox{there exists}\ g\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}\ \mbox{such that}\ f(y)-f(0)=\int_{0}^{y}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right\}.

For any
f_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote the
L^{2}
-scalar product by

(f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(y)f_{2}(y){\rm d}y.

Let
\chi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto[0,1]
be a non-increasing
C^{\infty}
function such that

\chi_{|(-\infty,1)}\equiv 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\chi_{|(2,\infty)}\equiv 0.

We see from the explicit expression of
Q
in Section
1.1
that, as
y\to\infty
,

Q(y)=c_{Q}e^{-y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q^{\prime}(y)=-c_{Q}e^{-y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right).

(1.7)

We also see that, as
y\to-\infty
,

Q(y)=c_{Q}e^{y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q^{\prime}(y)=c_{Q}e^{y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right).

(1.8)

Here, we denote
c_{Q}=12^{\frac{1}{4}}>0
by a universal positive constant. Moreover, we set

m_{0}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(y){\rm d}y>0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\alpha=\frac{c_{Q}}{m_{0}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{y}Q^{5}(y){\rm d}y>0.

(1.9)

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and smooth real-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we denote

\partial_{y}^{\leq n}f=\left(f,\partial_{y}f,\dots,\partial_{y}^{n}f\right),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

In addition, for any parameter
\mu\in(0,1)
and such function
f
, we set

f_{1+\mu}(y)=(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}f\big((1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}y\big),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

We now introduce the scaling operator and the linearized operator around
Q
:

\Lambda f=\frac{1}{2}f+yf^{\prime}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \mathcal{L}f=-f^{\prime\prime}+f-5Q^{4}f,\quad\mbox{for all}\ f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

(1.10)

From the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, for any parameter
\mu\in(0,1)
and smooth real-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,

f_{1+\mu}=f+\frac{\mu}{2}\Lambda f+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\left(\frac{(\Lambda^{2}f)_{1+s\mu}}{2(1+s\mu)^{2}}-\frac{(\Lambda f)_{1+s\mu}}{(1+s\mu)^{2}}\right){\rm d}s.

(1.11)

For a given small constant
\delta
, we denote by
\varrho(\delta)
a generic small constant with

\varrho(\delta)\to 0,\quad\mbox{as}\ \ \delta\to 0.

2.
Construction of the approximate solution

In this section, we construct the approximate solution for (
1.4
) with an explicit two-bubble asymptotic behavior and extract the evolution system of the geometrical parameters for the two bubbles. Let
I=[s_{0},s_{1}]\subset(0,\infty)
be an time interval. Recall that, we consider the following geometric parameters:

\mathcal{G}=(\lambda,z,\mu,x_{1},b_{1},b_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}.

Here, the function
\lambda
is the scaling parameter, the function
z
is the distance between the two bubbles, the function
\mu
is the difference of the scaling for the two bubbles, the function
x_{1}
is the position of the first bubble and the functions
(b_{1},b_{2})
are some parameters related to the non-localized profile
X
(see Section
2.3
for more details).

To state the general results on the solutions of (
1.4
) close to the sum of two decoupled bubbles, we assume that the function
\mathcal{G}(s)
satisfies the following estimates:

0<\lambda\ll 1,\quad 1\ll z<\infty,\quad|\mu|+|b_{1}|+|b_{2}|\ll 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad|\mu|z^{2}\ll 1.

(2.1)

For any smooth real-valued function
f:I\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we set

\left(\Gamma f\right)(s,y)=f_{1+\mu}(s,y-z(s))=(1+\mu(s))^{\frac{1}{4}}f(s,(1+\mu(s))^{\frac{1}{2}}(y-z(s))).

(2.2)

To simplify the notation, we denote

Q_{1}(s,y)=Q(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma Q)(s,y).

(2.3)

In addition, we set

S=Q_{1}-Q_{2},\quad G=S^{5}-Q_{1}^{5}+Q_{2}^{5}\quad\mbox{and}\quad H=5Q_{1}Q_{2}^{4}-5Q_{1}^{4}Q_{2}.

2.1.
The linearized operator

In this subsection, we recall some standard properties for the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
. We start with the following spectral theory.

Proposition 2.1

(Spectral theory of
\mathcal{L}
)
.

The operator
\mathcal{L}
defined by (
1.10
) is self-adjoint on
L^{2}
. Moreover, the operator
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

Spectrum of
\mathcal{L}
.
The operator
\mathcal{L}
has only one negative eigenvalue
-8
with
an positive radially symmetric eigenfunction
Q^{3}
and the kernel of
\mathcal{L}
is given by
{\rm{Ker}}\mathcal{L}={\rm{Span}}\{Q^{\prime}\}
.

(ii)

Scaling identities.
It holds

\mathcal{L}\Lambda Q=-2Q\quad\mbox{and}\quad(\Lambda Q,Q)=0.

(iii)

Coercivity of
\mathcal{L}
.

For all
f\in H^{1}
, we have

\left(f,Q^{\prime}\right)=\left(f,Q^{3}\right)=0\Longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{L}f,f\right)\geq\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

In addition, there exists
\nu>0
such that for all
f\in H^{1}
,

\left(\mathcal{L}f,f\right)\geq\nu\|f\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}\big(\left(f,Q\right)^{2}+\left(f,Q^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(f,\Lambda Q\right)^{2}\big).

(iv)

Inversion of
\mathcal{L}
.

For any
g\in L^{2}
with
(g,Q^{\prime})=0
, there exists a unique function
f\in H^{2}
with
(f,Q^{\prime})=0
such that
\mathcal{L}f=g
. Moreover, if
g\in\mathcal{Y}
, then we have
f\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

The proof is based on a standard argument related to the structure of
\mathcal{L}
and some results in Weinstein
[
47
]
. We refer to
[
29
, Lemma 2]
for details of the proof.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Let
g=Q^{4}
in (iv) of Proposition
2.1
.
It is easy to check that
(Q^{4},Q^{\prime})=0
, and thus,
there exists a unique smooth function
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
such that

\mathcal{L}Y=Q^{4}\quad\mbox{and}\quad(Y,Q^{\prime})=0.

Moreover, from (ii) of Proposition
2.1
, we compute

(Y,Q)=-\frac{1}{2}(Y,\mathcal{L}\Lambda Q)=-\frac{1}{2}\left(Q^{4},\Lambda Q\right)=-\frac{3}{5}m_{0}.

The function
Y
is related to the existence of the resonance for the operator
\partial_{y}\mathcal{L}
of the form
1+f
where
f
is a Schwartz function. More precisely, we check that

\mathcal{L}(1+5Y)=1\Longrightarrow(\mathcal{L}(1+5Y))^{\prime}=0.

On the other hand, by integration by parts, we compute

Z=(yQ^{4})^{\prime}\Longrightarrow(W,Q)=(Y,Q)+(Z,Q)=\frac{1}{5}m_{0}\quad\mbox{with}\ \ W=Y+Z.

To simplify the notation, we denote

\displaystyle Y_{1}(s,y)=Y(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Y_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma Y)(s,y),

\displaystyle Z_{1}(s,y)=Z(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Z_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma Z)(s,y).

We now introduce the existence of some non-localized profiles. We mention here that, the following non-localized profiles will be used to construct the approximate solution for (
1.4
) and the proof is inspired by
[
33
, Proposition 2.2]
.

Lemma 2.3

(Non-localized profiles)
.

(i)
Suppose that
g\in\mathcal{Y}
with
(g,Q)=0
. Then there exists a unique smooth function
f_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}_{1}
such that

\left(\mathcal{L}f_{1}\right)^{\prime}=g,\quad(f_{1},Q^{\prime})=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{y\to-\infty}f_{1}(y)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

(ii)
Suppose that
g_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}
with
(g_{1},Q)=0
. Then there exists a unique smooth function
f_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}f_{2}\right)^{\prime}=g_{1},\quad\left(f_{2},Q^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(f_{2}(y)-\left(\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}g_{1}(\sigma)\right)y\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to-\infty}\left(f_{2}(y)-\left(\lim_{\sigma\to-\infty}g_{1}(\sigma)\right)y\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

Proof.

Proof of (i). We consider
f_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of the form

f_{1}(y)=h(y)-\int_{y}^{\infty}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\quad\mbox{with}\ \ h\in\mathcal{Y}.

By an elementary computation, we see that
\left(\mathcal{L}f_{1}\right)^{\prime}=g
is equivalent to

\left(\mathcal{L}h\right)^{\prime}=g+\left(\mathcal{L}\int_{y}^{\infty}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right)^{\prime}=q^{\prime},\quad\mbox{with}\ \ q=g^{\prime}-5Q^{4}\int_{y}^{\infty}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

Note that, from
g\in\mathcal{Y}
and
(g,Q)=0
, we find

q\in\mathcal{Y}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left(q,Q^{\prime}\right)=-(q^{\prime},Q)=-(g,Q)+\left(\int_{y}^{\infty}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\mathcal{L}Q^{\prime}\right)=0.

Therefore, from (iv) of Proposition
2.1
, there exists a unique
h\in\mathcal{Y}
such that

\mathcal{L}h=q\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ (h,Q^{\prime})=0\Longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{L}h\right)^{\prime}=q^{\prime}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ (h,Q^{\prime})=0.

Then, from
h\in\mathcal{Y}
and the definition of
f_{1}
, we find
f_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}_{1}
and

\left(\mathcal{L}f_{1}\right)^{\prime}=g,\quad(f_{1},Q^{\prime})=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{y\to-\infty}f_{1}(y)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

Last, the uniqueness of
f_{1}
is a consequence of the uniqueness of
h
.

Proof of (ii). Similarly, we consider
f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of the form

f_{2}(y)=h_{1}(y)+\int_{0}^{y}g_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\quad\mbox{with}\ \ h_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}.

By an elementary computation, we see that
\left(\mathcal{L}f_{2}\right)^{\prime}=g_{1}
is equivalent to

\left(\mathcal{L}h_{1}\right)^{\prime}=g_{1}-\left(\mathcal{L}\int_{0}^{y}g_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right)^{\prime}=q_{1}^{\prime},\quad\mbox{with}\ \ q_{1}=g_{1}^{\prime}+5Q^{4}\int_{0}^{y}g_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

Note that, from
g_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and
(g_{1},Q)=0
, we find

q_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left(q_{1},Q^{\prime}\right)=-(q_{1}^{\prime},Q)=-(g_{1},Q)-\left(\int_{0}^{y}g_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\mathcal{L}Q^{\prime}\right)=0.

Using again (iv) of Proposition
2.1
, there exists a unique
h_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}
such that

\mathcal{L}h_{1}=q_{1}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ (h_{1},Q^{\prime})=0\Longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{L}h_{1}\right)^{\prime}=q_{1}^{\prime}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ (h_{1},Q^{\prime})=0.

Then, from
h_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}
and the definition of
f_{2}
, we find

{f}_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}
,

\left(\mathcal{L}f_{2}\right)^{\prime}=g_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left(f_{2},Q^{\prime}\right)=0.

In particular, from the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, we obtain

\int_{0}^{y}g_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma=\left.\sigma g_{1}(\sigma)\right|_{0}^{y}-\int_{0}^{y}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma=g_{1}(y)y-\int_{0}^{y}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,

which directly implies

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(f_{2}(y)-\left(\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}g_{1}(\sigma)\right)y\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to-\infty}\left(f_{2}(y)-\left(\lim_{\sigma\to-\infty}g_{1}(\sigma)\right)y\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

Last, the uniqueness of
f_{2}
is a consequence of the uniqueness of
h_{1}
.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

Note that, from the construction of
f_{1}
in (i) of Lemma
2.3
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist some constants
(c_{k},C_{k})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
such that

\left|\left(f_{1}(y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right)^{(k)}\right|\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)+\big|f_{1}^{(k)}(y)\big|\textbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)\leq C_{k}\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|}.

Similarly, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist some constants
(c_{k},C_{k})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\left(f_{2}(y)-\left(\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}g_{1}(\sigma)\right)y+\int_{0}^{\infty}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right)^{(k)}\right|\textbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(f_{2}(y)-\left(\lim_{\sigma\to-\infty}g_{1}(\sigma)\right)y-\int_{-\infty}^{0}\sigma g^{\prime}_{1}(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right)^{(k)}\right|\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|}.

Remark 2.5
.

Let
g=\Lambda Q
in (i) of Lemma
2.3
. Then there exists a unique smooth function
P\in\mathcal{Y}_{1}
such that

\left(\mathcal{L}P\right)^{\prime}=\Lambda Q,\quad(P,Q^{\prime})=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{y\to-\infty}P(y)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma.

In addition, from the definition of
\Lambda
and
P
, we compute
1
1

1

See
[
33
, Proposition 2.2]
for more detail.

\lim_{y\to-\infty}P(y)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma=2m_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad(P,Q)=\frac{1}{16}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(y){\rm d}y\right)^{2}=m_{0}^{2}.

Denote
X=P-2m_{0}(1+5Y)\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}
.
Using Remark
2.2
and Remark
2.4
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists some constants
(c_{k},C_{k})\in(0,\infty)^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\big|X^{(k)}(y)\big|\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)+\big|\left(X(y)+2m_{0}\right)^{(k)}\big|\textbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|}.

Using again Remark
2.2
, we compute

(X,Q)=(P,Q)-2m_{0}(1+5Y,Q)=-m_{0}^{2}.

Note that, from the uniqueness in (i) of Lemma
2.3
, we find

P(y)=-X(-y)\Longrightarrow X+P\ \ \mbox{is odd function}\Longrightarrow(X+P,\Lambda Q)=0.

To simplify the notation, we denote

\displaystyle P_{1}(s,y)=P(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad P_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma P)(s,y),

(2.4)

\displaystyle X_{1}(s,y)=X(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad X_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma X)(s,y).

To compute the leading-order interaction terms between
X
and
Q
, we rely on the following refined pointwise estimate for
X
(see Lemma
2.13
for more detail).

Lemma 2.6
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\left(X(y)+\frac{c_{Q}}{4}y^{2}e^{-|y|}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(X(y)+2m_{0}-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}y^{2}e^{-|y|}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|}.

Proof.

First, from (
1.7
), we check that
2
2

2

Here, we use the fact that
-f^{\prime\prime}+f=g\Longrightarrow|f(y)|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-|y-\sigma|}|g(\sigma)|{\rm d}\sigma
.

-Y^{\prime\prime}+Y=5Q^{4}Y+Q^{4}\Longrightarrow|Y^{(k)}(y)|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-|y-\sigma|}e^{-4|\sigma|}{\rm d}\sigma\lesssim e^{-|y|}.

(2.5)

Second, by an elementary computation, we also check that

\mathcal{L}\left(yQ^{\prime}\right)=-2Q^{\prime\prime}\Longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\bar{h}=yQ^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{\prime}\ \ \mbox{with}\ \bar{h}=-\frac{1}{4}y^{2}Q^{\prime}.

(2.6)

Using again (
1.7
) and (
1.8
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\left(y^{2}Q^{\prime}-c_{Q}y^{2}e^{-|y|}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(y^{2}Q^{\prime}+c_{Q}y^{2}e^{-|y|}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-|y|}.

It follows from the definition of
\bar{h}
that

\displaystyle\left|\left(\bar{h}(y)+\frac{c_{Q}}{4}y^{2}e^{-|y|}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\bar{h}(y)-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}y^{2}e^{-|y|}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|}.

Recall that, from the proof for (i) of Lemma
2.3
, we consider
P
of the form

P(y)=h(y)-\int_{y}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Here, the real-valued function
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a solution of

\mathcal{L}h=(\Lambda Q)^{\prime}-5Q^{4}\int_{y}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Based on the above identity and (
2.6
), we have

\mathcal{L}(h-\bar{h})=Q^{\prime}-5Q^{4}\int_{y}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

which directly implies that

\left|(h(y)-\bar{h}(y))^{(k)}\right|\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-|y-\sigma|}e^{-|y|}{\rm d}y\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|}.

(2.8)

On the other hand, from the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus,

\displaystyle\left|\left(\int^{\infty}_{y}\Lambda Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\int^{\infty}_{y}\Lambda Q(\sigma){\rm d}\sigma+2m_{0}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y|)e^{-|y|}.

Combining (
2.7
)–(
2.9
) with the definition of
X
, we complete the proof.
∎

Based on Lemma
2.3
, we consider the following two auxiliary functions that will be used to construct the approximate solution for (
1.4
). More precisely, these functions will help us to refine the leading-order term of the nonlinear interaction.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exist smooth functions
(A,B)\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}\times\mathcal{Z}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}A)^{\prime}+5c_{Q}(e^{y}Q^{4})^{\prime}+\alpha X=(\mathcal{L}B)^{\prime}-5c_{Q}(e^{-y}Q^{4})^{\prime}-\alpha X-10\alpha m_{0}W

\displaystyle=0.

In addition, we have
(A,Q^{\prime})=(B,Q^{\prime})=0
and there exist some universal constants
(a_{0},a_{1})\in\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, depending only on
P
and
Q
, such that

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(A(y)-2\alpha m_{0}y-a_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(B(y)+2\alpha m_{0}y+a_{1}\right)=0.

Moreover, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left(\left|A^{(k)}(y)\right|+\left|B^{(k)}(y)\right|\right)\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(A(y)-2\alpha m_{0}y-a_{0}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(B(y)+2\alpha m_{0}y+a_{1}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|}.

Proof.

Note that, from Remark
2.2
, Remark
2.5
and the definition of
\alpha
in (
1.9
),

\left(5c_{Q}(e^{y}Q^{4})^{\prime}+\alpha X,Q\right)=\left(5c_{Q}(e^{-y}Q^{4})^{\prime}+\alpha X+10\alpha m_{0}W,Q\right)=0.

Here, we use the fact that

\big(\big(e^{y}Q^{4}\big)^{\prime},Q\big)=-\big(\big(e^{-y}Q^{4}\big)^{\prime},Q\big)=\frac{1}{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{y}Q^{5}(y){\rm d}y.

Therefore, the existence of
(A,B)\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}\times\mathcal{Z}_{2}

is a direct consequence for (ii) of Lemma
2.3
. In addition, using Remark
2.5
, we compute

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\pm\infty}\left(5c_{Q}(e^{y}Q^{4})^{\prime}+\alpha X\right)

\displaystyle=-\alpha m_{0}\mp\alpha m_{0},

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\pm\infty}\left(5c_{Q}(e^{-y}Q^{4})^{\prime}+\alpha X+10\alpha m_{0}W\right)

\displaystyle=-\alpha m_{0}\mp\alpha m_{0}.

On the other hand, the resonance
1+5Y
satisfies
(\mathcal{L}(1+5Y))^{\prime}=0
, and thus, we could use such resonance to adjust the asymptotic behavior of
A
and
B
on the left side of the real axis
\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, using the above two identities and Remark
2.4
, we complete the proof for the asymptotics behavior of
A
and
B
as
y\to\pm\infty
.
∎

Similar to before, we also denote

\displaystyle A_{1}(s,y)=A(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad B_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma B)(s,y).

(2.10)

In addition, we consider the following two auxiliary functions that will be used to refine the leading-order terms of the ODE system for
(b_{1},b_{2})
.

Lemma 2.8
.

There exist smooth functions
(E,F)\in\mathcal{Z}_{2}\times\mathcal{Z}_{2}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}E)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Lambda X-2X+10(X^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}F)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2X-\Lambda X+10m_{0}(2Y-\Lambda Y)-10(P^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime}+15m_{0}Z.

In addition, we have
(E,Q^{\prime})=(F,Q^{\prime})=0
and there exist some universal constants
(a_{2},a_{3})\in\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, depending only on
P
and
Q
, such that

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(E(y)-3m_{0}y-a_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(F(y)+3m_{0}y+a_{3}\right)=0.

Moreover, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left(\left|E^{(k)}(y)\right|+\left|F^{(k)}(y)\right|\right)\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(E(y)-3m_{0}y-a_{2}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|},

\displaystyle\left|\left(F(y)+3m_{0}y+a_{3}\right)^{(k)}\right|\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{c_{k}}e^{-|y|}.

Proof.

From Remark
2.5
and integrating by parts, we check that

(\Lambda X,Q)=-(X,\Lambda Q)=(X,X^{\prime\prime\prime}-X^{\prime})-10((X^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime},X).

Based on the above identity, we compute

(\Lambda X+10(X^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime},Q)=-\frac{1}{2}\lim_{y\to\infty}X^{2}(y)=-2m_{0}^{2}=2(X,Q),

which implies that

\left(\Lambda X-2X+10(X^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime},Q\right)=0.

(2.11)

Similar as above, we check that

\left(\Lambda P+10(P^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime},Q\right)=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{y\to-\infty}P^{2}(y)=2m_{0}^{2}.

Then, using the definition of
X=P-2m_{0}(1+5Y)
, we find

\left(\Lambda P,Q\right)=(\Lambda X+10m_{0}\Lambda Y,Q)+4m_{0}^{2},

which implies that

(\Lambda X+10m_{0}\Lambda Y+10(P^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime},Q)=-2m_{0}^{2}.

On the other hand, using again Remark
2.2
and Remark
2.5
,

(2X+20m_{0}Y+15m_{0}Z,Q)=-2m_{0}^{2}.

Combining the above two identities, we obtain

\left(2X-\Lambda X+10m_{0}(2Y-\Lambda Y)-10(P^{2}Q^{3})^{\prime}+15m_{0}Z,Q\right)=0.

(2.12)

Note that, the proof of Lemma
2.8
follows directly from equations (
2.11
)–(
2.12
) and an argument similar to that used for Lemma
2.7
.
∎

Similar to before, we also denote

\displaystyle E_{1}(s,y)=E(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad F_{2}(s,y)=(\Gamma F)(s,y).

(2.13)

2.2.
Leading order of the nonlinear interaction

In this subsection, we study the interactions between the solitons and non-localized profiles which were introduced in Section
2.1
. We start with the following two technical Lemmas.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(\theta,K_{1},K_{2})\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
D\gg 1
. Assume that
f_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})

and
f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})

satisfy

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}(y)\right|+\left|f_{1}^{\prime}(y)\right|\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{K_{1}}e^{-\theta|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\left|f_{2}(y)\right|+\left|f_{2}^{\prime}(y)\right|\lesssim\left(1+|y|\right)^{K_{2}}e^{-\theta|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Then, for all
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
r=z_{2}-z_{1}>D
, we have

\displaystyle|\left(f_{1}(\cdot-z_{1}),f_{2}(\cdot-z_{2})\right)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(|z_{1}|^{K_{1}+K_{2}+1}+|z_{2}|^{K_{1}+K_{2}+1}\right)e^{-\theta r},

\displaystyle\left\|f_{1}(\cdot-z_{1})f_{2}(\cdot-z_{2})\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(|z_{1}|^{K_{1}+K_{2}+\frac{1}{2}}+|z_{2}|^{K_{1}+K_{2}+\frac{1}{2}}\right)e^{-\theta r}.

Proof.

First, using (
2.14
), for any
y<z_{1}
, we find

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right|+\left|\left(f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\theta r}\left(\langle y-z_{1}\rangle^{K_{2}}+|z_{2}|^{K_{2}}\right)\left(|f_{1}(y-z_{1})|+|f^{\prime}_{1}(y-z_{1})|\right).

Similarly, for any
y>z_{2}
, we find

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right|+\left|\left(f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\theta r}\left(\langle y-z_{2}\rangle^{K_{1}}+|z_{1}|^{K_{1}}\right)\left(|f_{2}(y-z_{2})|+|f^{\prime}_{2}(y-z_{2})|\right).

Then, using again (
2.14
), for any
z_{1}<y<z_{2}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right|+\left|\left(f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)^{\prime}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\theta r}\langle y-z_{1}\rangle^{K_{1}}\langle y-z_{2}\rangle^{K_{2}}\lesssim\left(|z_{1}|^{K_{1}+K_{2}}+|z_{2}|^{K_{1}+K_{2}}\right)e^{-\theta r}.

Combining above estimates and then integrating over
\mathbb{R}
, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\left(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
D\gg 1
. Let
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
r=z_{2}-z_{1}>D
.
Assume that
f_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\left|f_{1}(y)\right|+|f_{1}^{\prime}(y)|+|f_{2}(y)|+|f^{\prime}_{2}(y)|\lesssim e^{-|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(2.15)

Then the following estimates hold.

(i)

First-type estimate.

Suppose that
f_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle f_{1}(y)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}e^{-y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right),\quad\ \ \mbox{as}\ y\to\infty,

(2.16)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{1}(y)

\displaystyle=-\kappa_{1}e^{-y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right),\quad\mbox{as}\ y\to\infty.

Then, on
y>\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}e^{-r}\left(e^{-(y-z_{2})}f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)

\displaystyle+O_{H^{1}\left({z_{1}+z_{2}}<2y<\infty\right)}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}r}\right).

(ii)

Second-type estimate.

Suppose that
f_{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle f_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}e^{y}+O\left(e^{2y}\right),\quad\mbox{as}\ y\to-\infty,

(2.17)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}e^{y}+O\left(e^{2y}\right),\quad\mbox{as}\ y\to-\infty.

Then, on
y<\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}e^{-r}\left(e^{y-z_{1}}f_{1}(y-z_{1})\right)

\displaystyle+O_{H^{1}\left(-\infty<2y<z_{1}+z_{2}\right)}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}r}\right).

Proof.

Proof of (i). Using (
2.16
), for any
y>\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2}
, we find

y-z_{1}>\frac{z_{2}-z_{1}}{2}>\frac{D}{2}\Longrightarrow f_{1}(y-z_{1})=\kappa_{1}e^{-(y-z_{1})}+O\left(e^{-2(y-z_{1})}\right).

It follows from (
2.15
) that

\displaystyle f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}e^{-r}\left(e^{-(y-z_{2})}f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)

\displaystyle+O\left(\exp\left(-2(y-z_{1})-|y-z_{2}|\right)\right).

Based on a similar argument as above, we also find

\displaystyle\left(f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}e^{-r}\left(e^{-(y-z_{2})}f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle+O\left(\exp\left(-2(y-z_{1})-|y-z_{2}|\right)\right).

By
r=z_{2}-z_{1}>D\gg 1
and change of variable, we compute

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-4(y-z_{1})-2|y-z_{2}|\right){\rm d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{-\frac{r}{2}}^{\infty}\exp\left(-4y-2|y|-4r\right){\rm d}y=O\left(e^{-3r}\right).

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof of first-type estimate.

Proof of (ii). Using (
2.17
), for any
y<\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2}
, we find

y-z_{2}<-\frac{z_{2}-z_{1}}{2}<-\frac{D}{2}\Longrightarrow f_{2}(y-z_{2})=\kappa_{2}e^{y-z_{2}}+O\left(e^{2(y-z_{2})}\right).

It follows from (
2.15
) that

\displaystyle f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}e^{-r}\left(e^{y-z_{1}}f_{1}(y-z_{1})\right)

\displaystyle+O\left(\exp\left(2(y-z_{2})-|y-z_{1}|\right)\right).

Based on a similar argument as above, we also find

\displaystyle\left(f_{1}(y-z_{1})f_{2}(y-z_{2})\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}e^{-r}\left(e^{y-z_{1}}f_{1}(y-z_{1})\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle+O\left(\exp\left(2(y-z_{2})-|y-z_{1}|\right)\right).

By
r=z_{2}-z_{1}>D\gg 1
and change of variable, we compute

\displaystyle\int^{\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2}}_{-\infty}\exp\left(4(y-z_{2})-2|y-z_{1}|\right){\rm d}y

\displaystyle=\int^{\frac{r}{2}}_{-\infty}\exp\left(4y-2|y|-4r\right){\rm d}y=O\left(e^{-3r}\right).

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof of second-type estimate.
∎

Recall that, we denote

S=Q_{1}-Q_{2},\quad G=S^{5}-Q_{1}^{5}+Q_{2}^{5}\quad\mbox{and}\quad H=5Q_{1}Q_{2}^{4}-5Q_{1}^{4}Q_{2}.

We now study the leading-order terms for the nonlinear interactions
G
and
H
.

Lemma 2.11
.

The following estimates hold.

(i)

Bound
. We have

\left\|\partial_{y}G-\partial_{y}H\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim z^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2z}.

(ii)

Asymptotic
. We have

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}^{4}Q_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{Q}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\mu z\right)e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+O_{H^{1}}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}+|\mu|e^{-z}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}Q^{4}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{Q}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)+O_{H^{1}}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}+|\mu|e^{-z}\right).

In addition, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{y}H

\displaystyle=5c_{Q}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)+O_{H^{1}}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}\right)

\displaystyle-5c_{Q}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\mu z\right)e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+O_{H^{1}}\left(|\mu|e^{-z}\right).

Proof.

Proof of (i). By an elementary computation, we find

G-H=10Q_{1}^{3}Q_{2}^{2}-10Q_{1}^{2}Q_{2}^{3}.

It follows directly that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{y}G-\partial_{y}H\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\partial^{\leq 1}_{y}Q_{1}|^{3}|\partial^{\leq 1}_{y}Q_{2}|^{2}+|\partial^{\leq 1}_{y}Q_{1}|^{2}|\partial^{\leq 1}_{y}Q_{2}|^{3},

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{2}_{y}G-\partial_{y}^{2}H\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\partial^{\leq 2}_{y}Q_{1}|^{3}|\partial^{\leq 2}_{y}Q_{2}|^{2}+|\partial^{\leq 2}_{y}Q_{1}|^{2}|\partial^{\leq 2}_{y}Q_{2}|^{3}.

Recall that, from the explicit expression of
Q
in Section
1.1
, as
y\to\infty
,

Q(y)=c_{Q}e^{-y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q^{\prime}(y)=-c_{Q}e^{-y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right).

(2.18)

Recall that, from the explicit expression of
Q
in Section
1.1
, as
y\to-\infty
,

Q(y)=c_{Q}e^{y}+O\left(e^{2y}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q^{\prime}(y)=c_{Q}e^{y}+O\left(e^{-2y}\right).

(2.19)

Then, from (
1.11
), we check that

\displaystyle Q_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=Q(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}\Lambda Q(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{2}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}Q_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime}(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}(\Lambda Q)^{\prime}(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{2}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}Q_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime\prime}(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}(\Lambda Q)^{\prime\prime}(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{2}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right).

Therefore, from Lemma
2.9
and (
2.18
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}G-\partial_{y}H\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\mu|z^{\frac{5}{2}}e^{-2z}+\mu^{2}z^{\frac{9}{2}}e^{-2z}+\mu^{4}z^{\frac{17}{2}}e^{-2(1-|\mu|)z}

\displaystyle+|\mu|^{3}z^{\frac{13}{2}}e^{-2z}+\mu^{6}z^{\frac{25}{2}}e^{-2(1-|\mu|)z}+z^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2z}.

Combining the above estimate with (
2.1
), we complete the proof of (i).

Proof of (ii). Using again (
1.11
), (
2.1
) and Lemma
2.9
,

\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}^{4}Q_{2}\right)=\partial_{y}\left(Q^{4}(y)Q(y-z)\right)+\frac{\mu}{2}\partial_{y}(Q^{4}(y)(\Lambda Q)(y-z))+O_{H^{1}}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}\right).

Based on (ii) of Lemma
2.10
and (
2.18
)–(
2.19
), for
y<\frac{z}{2}
, we find

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\left(Q^{4}(y)Q(y-z)\right)

\displaystyle=c_{Q}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+O_{H^{1}(-\infty<2y<z)}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}(Q^{4}(y)(\Lambda Q)(y-z))

\displaystyle=-c_{Q}ze^{-z}\partial_{y}(e^{y}Q^{4}(y))+O_{H^{1}(-\infty<2y<z)}\left(e^{-z}\right),

Here, we use the fact that

\displaystyle(\Lambda Q)(y-z)

\displaystyle=-zQ^{\prime}(y-z)+yQ^{\prime}(y-z)+\frac{1}{2}Q(y-z)

\displaystyle=-c_{Q}ze^{y-z}+O\left(e^{2(y-z)}+(1+|y|)e^{y-z}\right),\quad\mbox{for}\ \ y<\frac{z}{2}.

Then, from the exponential decay of
Q
, we directly have

\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q^{4}(y)Q(y-z)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}(z<2y<\infty)}+\left\|e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}(z<2y<\infty)}\lesssim e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}.

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof for the first estimate.

Based on (i) of Lemma
2.10
and (
2.18
), for
y>\frac{z}{2}
, we find

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\left(Q(y)Q^{4}(y-z)\right)

\displaystyle=c_{Q}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)+O_{H^{1}(z<2y<\infty)}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}\right).

Thus, using a similar argument, we complete the proof for the second estimate. Combining the first and second estimates, we complete the proof for the third one, and thus, the proof of (ii) is complete.
∎

Since the functions
(X,A,B,E,F)
are non-localized profiles, the following estimates related to the interactions between solitons and such profiles are also needed.

Lemma 2.12
.

The following estimates hold for some constant
K>1
.

(i)

Estimates related to
X
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{2}^{4}(X_{1}+2m_{0})\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{2}^{3}(X_{1}+2m_{0})\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}^{3}X_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}^{4}X_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle|(Q_{1},X_{2})|+|(\partial_{y}Q_{1},X_{2})|

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z},

\displaystyle\left\|\left(Q_{1}^{3}X_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\left(Q_{1}^{4}X_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z}.

(ii)

Estimates related to
A
and
B
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}^{4}B_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{2}^{4}\left(A_{1}-2\alpha m_{0}y-a_{0}\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z}.

(iii)

Estimates related to
E
and
F
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{1}^{4}F_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{y}\left(Q_{2}^{4}\left(E_{1}-3m_{0}y-a_{2}\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim z^{K}e^{-z}.

Proof.

Proof of (i). First, using (
2.1
) and Remark
2.5
,
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that for any
y<\frac{z}{2}
, we have

\big|X^{(k)}_{2}(y)\big|\lesssim\left((1+|y|)^{c_{k}}+z^{c_{k}}\right)e^{-z}e^{-(1-|\mu|)^{\frac{1}{2}}y}.

Then, using again the exponential decay of
Q
in (
1.7
), for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y>\frac{z}{2}
,

\big|Q^{(k)}_{1}(y)\big|\lesssim\big|Q^{(k)}(y)\big|\lesssim e^{-y}\lesssim e^{-z}e^{-(y-z)}.

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof for the first estimate.

On the other hand, using again (
1.7
) and (
2.1
), for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y<\frac{z}{2}
,

\big|Q^{(k)}_{2}(y)\big|\lesssim e^{-(1-|\mu|)^{\frac{1}{2}}z}e^{(1-{|\mu|})^{\frac{1}{2}}y}\lesssim e^{-z}e^{(1-|\mu|)^{\frac{1}{2}}y}.

Then, using again Remark
2.5
,
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that for any
y>\frac{z}{2}
, we have

\big|\left(X_{1}+2m_{0}\right)^{(k)}(y)\big|\lesssim(1+|y|)^{c_{k}}e^{-y}\lesssim\left((1+|y-z|)^{c_{k}}+z^{c_{k}}\right)e^{-z}e^{-(y-z)}.

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof for the second estimate.

Proof of (ii). Recall that, from Lemma
2.7
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that for any
y<\frac{z}{2}
, we have

\big|B^{(k)}_{2}(y)\big|\lesssim\left((1+|y|)^{c_{k}}+z^{c_{k}}\right)e^{-z}e^{-(1-|\mu|)^{\frac{1}{2}}y}.

Recall also that, from Lemma
2.7
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}>0
such that for any
y>\frac{z}{2}
, we have

\big|(A_{1}-2\alpha m_{0}y-a_{0})^{(k)}(y)\big|\lesssim(1+|y|)^{c_{k}}e^{-y}\lesssim\left((1+|y-z|)^{c_{k}}+z^{c_{k}}\right)e^{-z}e^{-(y-z)}.

Hence, using a similar argument as above, we complete the proof for (ii).
∎

Lemma 2.13
.

The following refined estimates hold.

(i)

Refined estimate related to
X_{1}
. We have

\displaystyle\partial_{y}(X_{1}Q_{2}^{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)

\displaystyle-2m_{0}\partial_{y}(Q_{2}^{4})+O_{H^{1}}(ze^{-z}+|\mu|z^{3}e^{-z}).

(ii)

Refined estimate related to
X_{2}
. We have

\partial_{y}(X_{2}Q_{1}^{4})=-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+O_{H^{1}}(ze^{-z}+|\mu|z^{3}e^{-z}).

Proof.

Proof of (i). Recall that, from (
1.11
), we find

\displaystyle Q_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=Q(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}\Lambda Q(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{2}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}Q_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime}(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}(\Lambda Q)^{\prime}(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{2}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}Q_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime\prime}(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}(\Lambda Q)^{\prime\prime}(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{2}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right).

Then, from Lemma
2.6
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y>0
, we check that

\displaystyle X_{1}^{(k)}(y)

\displaystyle=\left(-2m_{0}+\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-y}\right)^{(k)}+O\left((1+|y-z|+z)e^{-y}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{Q}}{4}\left(\left(y-z)^{2}e^{-y}\right)\right)^{(k)}+\frac{c_{Q}}{2}\left(z(y-z)e^{-y}\right)^{(k)}.

Based on the above estimate and Lemma
2.10
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{y}(X_{1}Q_{2}^{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)

(2.20)

\displaystyle-2m_{0}\partial_{y}(Q_{2}^{4})+O_{H^{1}(0,\infty)}(ze^{-z}+|\mu|z^{3}e^{-z}).

Here, we use the fact that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-|y|}\left(1+|y-z|+|y-z|^{2}\right)e^{-4|y-z|}{\rm d}y\lesssim e^{-z}.

On the other hand, using again the exponential decay of
Q
, we directly have

\displaystyle\partial_{y}(X_{1}Q_{2}^{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)

\displaystyle-2m_{0}\partial_{y}(Q_{2}^{4})+O_{H^{1}(-\infty,0)}(ze^{-z}+|\mu|z^{3}e^{-z}).

Combining the above estimate with (
2.20
), we complete the proof for (i).

Proof of (ii). Using again (
1.11
) and Lemma
2.6
, for any
y<z
, we find

\displaystyle X_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=X(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}\Lambda X(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{4}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}X_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=X^{\prime}(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}(\Lambda X)^{\prime}(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{4}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}X_{2}(s,y)

\displaystyle=X^{\prime\prime}(y-z)+\frac{\mu}{2}(\Lambda X)^{\prime\prime}(y-z)+O\left(\mu^{2}(y-z)^{4}e^{-(1-|\mu|)|y-z|}\right).

Then, from Lemma
2.6
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
y<z
, we check that

\displaystyle X^{(k)}(y-z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}\left(z^{2}e^{(y-z)}\right)^{(k)}+O\left((1+|y|+z)e^{(y-z)}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}\left(y^{2}e^{(y-z)}\right)^{(k)}+\frac{c_{Q}}{2}\left(yze^{(y-z)}\right)^{(k)}.

Based on the above estimate and Lemma
2.10
, we obtain

\partial_{y}(X_{2}Q_{1}^{4})=-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+O_{H^{1}(-\infty,z)}(ze^{-z}+|\mu|z^{3}e^{-z}).

(2.21)

On the other hand, using again the exponential decay of
Q
, we directly have

\partial_{y}(X_{2}Q_{1}^{4})=-\frac{c_{Q}}{4}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+O_{H^{1}(z,\infty)}(ze^{-z}+|\mu|z^{3}e^{-z}).

Combining the above estimate with (
2.21
), we complete the proof for (ii).
∎

2.3.
Approximate solution

In this subsection, we construct the approximate two-bubble solution for (
1.4
). We now state the definition of admissible geometric parameters
\mathcal{G}=(\lambda,z,\mu,x_{1},b_{1},b_{2})\in(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
. From now on, we denote by
\Theta=b_{2}-b_{1}
to keep track of the size difference between
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
I=[s_{0},s_{1}]\subset(0,\infty)
be an time interval.
We say that the function
\mathcal{G}(s):I\mapsto(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
is admissible if it satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{7s\log s}\leq b_{1}\leq\frac{1}{5s\log s},\quad\frac{1}{7s\log s}\leq b_{2}\leq\frac{1}{5s\log s},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{7s^{2}\log s}\leq\alpha e^{-z}\leq\frac{1}{5s^{2}\log s}\quad\mbox{and}\quad s|\Theta|+|\mu|\leq\frac{10}{s}.

Note that, from the definition of admissible geometric parameters for
z
, we find

e^{-z}\in\left[\frac{1}{10\alpha s^{2}\log s},\frac{1}{\alpha s^{2}\log s}\right]\Longrightarrow|z-2\log s|\lesssim\log\log s.

(2.22)

To simplify the notation, for any admissible geometric function
\mathcal{G}(s)
, we denote
3
3

3

See (
2.4
) and (
2.10
) for the definition of
(X_{1},X_{2},A_{1},B_{2})

a=6b_{1}|\log b_{1}|\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ R=b_{1}X_{1}-\zeta_{2}X_{2}+e^{-z}(A_{1}+B_{2}).

(2.23)

Here, we denote
b_{2}=(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{2}}\zeta_{2}
. From Definition
2.14
, we compute

a=6b_{1}|\log b_{1}|\in\left[\frac{6}{7s},\frac{6}{5s}\right]\subseteq\Longrightarrow a^{-1}\in\left[\frac{5s}{6},\frac{7s}{6}\right].

(2.24)

Note that, the above-mentioned functions are non-localized profiles, and thus, we should consider the following suitable cut-off function

\phi(s,y)=\chi\left(a(s)y\right)\Longrightarrow\phi_{|(-\infty,a^{-1})}\equiv 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\phi_{|(2a^{-1},\infty)}\equiv 0.

(2.25)

In addition, we require the refined term
U=U_{1}+U_{2}
. More precisely, the term
U_{1}
is introduced to refined the interactions at the
b_{1}^{2}
level
4
4

4

See (
2.13
) for the definition of
(E_{1},F_{2})
.
:

U_{1}=b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\phi+5(3m_{0}z+a_{2})b_{1}^{2}Y_{2}.

(2.26)

We also need the following refined term
U_{2}
which is related to the interactions between the soliton
Q_{2}
and the non-localized profiles
X_{1}
and
A_{1}
:

U_{2}=5\left(2\alpha m_{0}z+a_{0}\right)e^{-z}Y_{2}-\frac{10m_{0}b_{1}Y_{2}}{(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}}.

(2.27)

We now introduce the approximate solution and the related error term,

V=S+R\phi+U\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \Psi(V)=\partial_{s}V+\partial_{y}\left(\partial_{y}^{2}V-V+V^{5}\right)+b_{1}\Lambda V.

(2.28)

To avoid abuse of notation, we also denote
V(s,y)=V(y;(z,\mu,b_{1},b_{2}))\in H^{1}
.

Remark 2.15
.

On the one hand, the first two terms in
R
are the standard blow-up profiles for the mass-critical gKdV equation, while the last two are related to the interaction between the solitons. On the other hand, the first term in
U_{1}
is used to refine the interaction at the
b_{1}^{2}
level, while the second one is related to the interaction between the soliton
Q_{2}
and non-localized profile
E_{1}
. Last, the first term in
U
is used to refine the interaction between the soliton
Q_{2}
and non-localized profile
A_{1}
, while the second one is related to the interaction between the soliton
Q_{2}
and non-localized profile
X_{1}
. See Proposition
2.18
for more detail.

We now introduce the estimates related to non-localized profiles.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
I=[s_{0},s_{1}]\subset(0,\infty)
be an time interval and
the function
\mathcal{G}(s):I\mapsto(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
be admissible. Then the following estimates hold.

(i)

Estimate for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
We have

\|(X_{1}-X_{2})\phi\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\log^{\frac{1}{2}}s.

(ii)

Estimate for
R
.
We have

\left\|(\partial_{y}R)(\partial^{2}_{y}\phi)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|R\partial^{3}_{y}\phi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\left(\partial^{2}_{y}R\right)(\partial_{y}\phi)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\frac{1}{s^{3}\log s}.

(iii)

Estimate for
A_{1}
and
B_{2}
.
We have

\|(A_{1}+B_{2})\phi\|_{H^{1}}+\|y(\partial_{y}(A_{1}+B_{2}))\phi\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim s^{\frac{1}{2}}\log s.

(iv)

First estimate for
E_{1}
and
F_{2}
.
We have

\|(E_{1}+F_{2})\phi\|_{H^{1}}+\|y(\partial_{y}(E_{1}+F_{2}))\phi\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim s^{\frac{1}{2}}\log s.

(v)

Second estimates for
E_{1}
and
F_{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\left\|(\partial_{y}E_{1}+\partial_{y}F_{2})(\partial^{2}_{y}\phi)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial^{3}_{y}\phi\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-1},

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\partial^{2}_{y}E_{1}+\partial_{y}^{2}F_{2}\right)(\partial_{y}\phi)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\|S^{4}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{y}\phi\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-1}.

(vi)

Third estimates for
E_{1}
and
F_{2}
.
We have

\left\|(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{y}\phi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|y(\partial_{y}(E_{1}+F_{2}))\partial_{y}\phi\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim s^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log s.

Proof.

Proof of (i). We first rewrite the term
X_{1}-X_{2}
by

X_{1}-X_{2}=(X_{1}+2m_{0})-\big(X_{2}+2(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}m_{0}\big)+2\big((1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}-1\big)m_{0}.

It follows from Remark
2.5
and Definition
2.14
that

\displaystyle|\partial_{y}^{\leq 1}(X_{1}-X_{2})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}+e^{-\frac{|y-z|}{2}}\big)\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(y)+\textbf{1}_{[0,z]}(y)

\displaystyle+\big(e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}+e^{-\frac{|y-z|}{2}}+s^{-1}\big)\textbf{1}_{(z,\infty)}(y).

Based on the above estimate and
\rm{supp}\phi\subset(-\infty,\frac{5s}{2})
, we complete the proof for (i).

Proof of (ii).
We first rewrite the term
R
by

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=e^{-z}\big(B_{2}+2\alpha m_{0}(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}(y-z)\big)+2\alpha m_{0}(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}ze^{-z}

\displaystyle+2b_{1}\big((1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}-1\big)m_{0}-b_{1}(X_{2}+2(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}m_{0})+(b_{1}-\zeta_{2})X_{2}

\displaystyle+b_{1}(X_{1}+2m_{0})+e^{-z}(A_{1}-2\alpha m_{0}y)+2\alpha m_{0}e^{-z}y\big(1-(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}\big).

It follows from (
2.22
), Remark
2.5
, Lemma
2.7
and Definition
2.14
that

|R|\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}(y)\lesssim\left(s^{-2}+(s^{-3}\log^{-1}s)y\right)\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}(y)\lesssim s^{-2}.

Moreover, we check that

\left(|\partial_{y}R|+|\partial^{2}_{y}R|+|\partial^{3}_{y}R|\right)\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}(y)\lesssim\frac{1}{s^{3}\log s}.

Second, from (
2.24
) and the definition of
\phi
in (
2.25
), we find

s|\partial_{y}\phi|+s^{2}|\partial_{y}^{2}\phi|+s^{3}|\partial_{y}^{3}\phi|+s^{4}|\partial_{y}^{4}\phi|\lesssim\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}(y).

(2.29)

Combining the above estimates, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|(\partial_{y}R)(\partial^{2}_{y}\phi)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|R\partial^{3}_{y}\phi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\left(\partial^{2}_{y}R\right)(\partial_{y}\phi)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\partial^{\leq 3}_{y}R|\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{2}_{y}\phi\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial^{\leq 3}_{y}R|\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial^{3}_{y}\phi\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{y}^{2}R\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{y}\phi\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{y}^{3}R\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{y}\phi\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\frac{1}{s^{3}\log s},

which directly completes the proof of (ii).

Proof of (iii).
We first rewrite the term
A_{1}+B_{2}
by

\displaystyle A_{1}+B_{2}

\displaystyle=(A_{1}-2\alpha m_{0}y)+2\alpha m_{0}y\big(1-(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}\big)

\displaystyle+(B_{2}+2\alpha m_{0}(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}(y-z))+2\alpha m_{0}(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}z.

It follows from Lemma
2.7
and Definition
2.14
that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(A_{1}+B_{2}\right)\phi\right\|_{H^{1}(z,\infty)}\lesssim\left\|\left(A_{1}+B_{2}\right)\phi\right\|_{H^{1}\left(z,\frac{5}{2}s\right)}\lesssim s^{\frac{1}{2}}\log s,

\displaystyle\|y(\partial_{y}(A_{1}+B_{2}))\phi\|_{H^{1}(z,\infty)}\lesssim\|y(\partial_{y}(A_{1}+B_{2}))\phi\|_{H^{1}\left(z,\frac{5}{2}s\right)}\lesssim s^{\frac{1}{2}}\log s.

Second, using again Lemma
2.7
and Definition
2.14
, on
[0,z]
, we find

\left|\partial_{y}^{\leq 2}A_{1}\right|+\left|\partial_{y}^{\leq 2}B_{2}\right|\lesssim(1+|y|)+\log s,

which directly implies that

\left\|\left(A_{1}+B_{2}\right)\phi\right\|_{H^{1}(0,z)}+\|y(\partial_{y}(A_{1}+B_{2}))\phi\|_{H^{1}(0,z)}\lesssim\log^{4}s.

Last, from the exponential decay of
A
and
B
on the left-hand side of
\mathbb{R}
,

\left\|\left(A_{1}+B_{2}\right)\phi\right\|_{H^{1}(-\infty,0)}+\|y(\partial_{y}(A_{1}+B_{2}))\phi\|_{H^{1}(-\infty,0)}\lesssim 1.

Combining the above estimates, we directly complete the proof of (iii).

Proof of (iv). We first rewrite the term
E_{1}+F_{2}
by

\displaystyle E_{1}+F_{2}

\displaystyle=(E_{1}-3m_{0}y)+3m_{0}y\big(1-(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}\big)

(2.30)

\displaystyle+(F_{2}+3m_{0}(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}(y-z))+3m_{0}(1+\mu)^{\frac{3}{4}}z.

Based on a similar argument as in (iii), we complete the proof for (iv).

Proof of (v). Note that, from (
2.30
) and Lemma
2.8
, we have

\left\|(E_{1}+F_{2})\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\log s.

In addition, we also have

\displaystyle\left(\left|\partial_{y}(E_{1}+F_{2})\right|+\left|\partial^{2}_{y}(E_{1}+F_{2})\right|\right)\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-1},

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{3}_{y}(E_{1}+F_{2})\right|+\left|\partial_{y}^{\leq 1}(S^{4}(E_{1}+F_{2}))\right|\textbf{1}_{[\frac{s}{2},\frac{5s}{2}]}(y)

\displaystyle\lesssim s^{-1}.

Based on a similar argument as in (ii), we complete the proof for (v).

Proof of (vi). The proof is similar to (iv), and we omit it.
∎

Consider the following functions which are related to the modulation equations:

\vec{{\rm{Mod}}}=\left(\dot{z}-b_{1}z-\mu,\vec{N},\dot{\mu}-2\Theta\right).

(2.31)

Here, we denote
\Theta=b_{2}-b_{1}
and

\vec{N}=\left(\dot{\zeta}_{2}+\alpha(1+\mu)e^{-z}+2b_{1}^{2},\dot{b}_{1}+\alpha e^{-z}+2b_{1}^{2}\right).

Definition 2.17
.

Let
I=[s_{0},s_{1}]\subset(0,\infty)
be a time interval and
the function
\mathcal{G}(s):I\mapsto(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
be admissible.
We denote by
\mathcal{S}
the set of smooth functions
f:I\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\|f(s)\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\frac{|\dot{z}|}{s^{2}\log s}+\frac{|\dot{\mu}|}{s^{\frac{3}{2}}\log s}+\frac{|\dot{b}_{1}|+|\dot{b}_{2}|}{s\log s}+\frac{1}{s^{3}\log s}.

In addition, for such smooth function
f\in\mathcal{S}
, we denote
f=O_{\mathcal{S}}(1)
.

To state the modulation equations, we also need the following specific functions:

\vec{M}Q=(M_{1Q},M_{2Q},M_{3Q},M_{4Q}).

(2.32)

Here, we set

\displaystyle\left(M_{1Q},M_{2Q}\right)=\left(\partial_{y}Q_{2},-X_{2}\phi\right)\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ M_{3Q}=X_{1}\phi-\frac{10m_{0}}{(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}}Y_{2}.

In addition, we denote

M_{4Q}=-\frac{1}{2(1+\mu)}\left(\Gamma(\Lambda Q)+\zeta_{2}\Gamma(\Lambda X)\phi-e^{-z}\Gamma(\Lambda B)\phi\right).

Recall that, from Definition
2.14
, we find

e^{-z}\in\left[\frac{1}{7\alpha s^{2}\log s},\frac{1}{5\alpha s^{2}\log s}\right]\Longrightarrow(b_{1}z,b_{2}z)\in\left[\frac{2}{7s},\frac{2}{5s}\right]^{2}.

The above estimates will be used frequently in the remainder of this article.

We now prove that the smooth real-valued function
V:I\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is an approximate solution of the rescaled equation (
1.4
) in the following sense.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
I=[s_{0},s_{1}]\subset(0,\infty)
be an time interval and
the function
\mathcal{G}(s):I\mapsto(0,\infty)^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
be admissible. Then we have

\Psi(V)=\vec{{\rm{Mod}}}\cdot\vec{M}Q+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Psi_{i}(V)+O_{\mathcal{S}}(1),

where

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(V)

\displaystyle=\left(\dot{z}-b_{1}z-\mu\right)(\zeta_{2}\partial_{y}X_{2}+10m_{0}b_{1}\partial_{y}Y_{2})

\displaystyle+b_{1}^{3}\Lambda\left((E_{1}+F_{2})\phi\right)-b_{1}(\zeta_{2}-b_{1})\Lambda X_{2}\phi-\Theta\zeta_{2}\Gamma(\Lambda X)\phi

\displaystyle+zb_{1}(\zeta_{2}-b_{1})\partial_{y}X_{2}\phi+b_{1}e^{-z}\left(\Lambda A_{1}+\Lambda B_{2}\right)\phi+\Theta e^{-z}\Gamma(\Lambda B)\phi,

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(V)

\displaystyle=-\dot{z}e^{-z}\left(A_{1}+B_{2}\right)\phi-10\alpha m_{0}\dot{z}ze^{-z}(Y_{2}+\partial_{y}Y_{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{5}{2}m_{0}b_{1}\dot{\mu}\left(Y_{2}-2\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\right)-\dot{z}e^{-z}\phi\partial_{y}B_{2}+30m_{0}z\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}Y_{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}\phi}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma(\Lambda F)+2\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}(E_{1}+F_{2})\phi-\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\phi\partial_{y}F_{2}+\mu e^{-z}\phi\partial_{y}B_{2},

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(V)

\displaystyle=\frac{5c_{Q}}{4}b_{1}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)+\mu b_{1}^{2}\phi\partial_{y}F_{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{5c_{Q}}{4}\zeta_{2}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}(e^{y}Q^{4}(y))+10\alpha m_{0}\mu ze^{-z}\partial_{y}Y_{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{5c_{Q}}{2}\mu ze^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{y}Q^{4}(y)\right)+10\alpha m_{0}z(1-(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}})e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(Q_{2}^{4}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}(V)=b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{s}\phi-b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{y}\phi+R\partial_{s}\phi+b_{1}yR\partial_{y}\phi-R\partial_{y}\phi.

Here, the approximate solution
V
and the error term
\Psi(V)
are defined by (
2.28
).

Proof.

Step 1.
General computation. First, from the definition of
\Gamma
in (
2.2
),
for any smooth real-valued function
f:I\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,

\partial_{s}\left(\Gamma f\right)=\Gamma\left(\partial_{s}f\right)-\dot{z}\partial_{y}\left(\Gamma f\right)+\frac{\dot{\mu}}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma\left(\Lambda f\right).

(2.33)

Based on the above identity and the definition of
Q_{2}
in (
2.3
), we compute

\partial_{s}Q_{2}=-\dot{z}\partial_{y}Q_{2}+\frac{\dot{\mu}}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma(\Lambda Q)\Longrightarrow\partial_{s}S=\dot{z}\partial_{y}Q_{2}-\frac{\dot{\mu}}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma(\Lambda Q).

(2.34)

Then, using again (
2.33
) and the definition of
R
in (
2.23
), we also compute

\displaystyle\partial_{s}(R\phi)

\displaystyle=\dot{b}_{1}X_{1}\phi-\dot{\zeta}_{2}X_{2}\phi-\dot{z}e^{-z}(A_{1}+B_{2})\phi+R\partial_{s}\phi

(2.35)

\displaystyle+\dot{z}(\zeta_{2}\partial_{y}X_{2}-e^{-z}\partial_{y}B_{2})\phi+\frac{\dot{\mu}}{2(1+\mu)}\left(e^{-z}\Gamma(\Lambda B)-\zeta_{2}\Gamma(\Lambda X)\right)\phi.

In addition, from (
2.26
), we check that

\displaystyle\partial_{s}U_{1}

\displaystyle=2\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}(E_{1}+F_{2})\phi-\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\phi\partial_{y}F_{2}+\frac{\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}\phi}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma(\Lambda F)

(2.36)

\displaystyle-5(3m_{0}z+a_{2})\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\partial_{y}Y_{2}+\frac{5(3m_{0}z+a_{2})}{2(1+\mu)}\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}\Gamma(\Lambda Y)

\displaystyle+b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{s}\phi+15m_{0}\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}Y_{2}+10(3m_{0}z+a_{2})\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}Y_{2}.

Similarly, from (
2.27
), we check that

\displaystyle\partial_{s}U_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{z}}{(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(5(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(2\alpha m_{0}z+a_{0}\right)e^{-z}-10m_{0}b_{1}\right)\partial_{y}Y_{2}

(2.37)

\displaystyle+\frac{\dot{\mu}}{2(1+\mu)^{\frac{5}{4}}}\left(5(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(2\alpha m_{0}z+a_{0}\right)e^{-z}-10m_{0}b_{1}\right)\Gamma(\Lambda Y)

\displaystyle-\frac{10m_{0}\dot{b}_{1}Y_{2}}{(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}}+\frac{5m_{0}b_{1}\dot{\mu}Y_{2}}{2(1+\mu)^{\frac{5}{4}}}-5a_{0}\dot{z}e^{-z}Y_{2}+10\alpha m_{0}\dot{z}e^{-z}(1-z)Y_{2}.

Combining identities (
2.34
)–(
2.37
) with the definition of
V
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{s}V

\displaystyle=\dot{z}M_{1Q}+\dot{\zeta}_{2}M_{2Q}+\dot{b}_{1}{M_{3Q}}+\dot{\mu}M_{4Q}

(2.38)

\displaystyle+\dot{z}\left(\zeta_{2}\phi\partial_{y}X_{2}+{10m_{0}b_{1}}\partial_{y}Y_{2}\right)+\mathcal{H}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{3}+\mathcal{H}_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{5}.

Here, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{5m_{0}b_{1}\dot{\mu}}{2(1+\mu)^{\frac{5}{4}}}\left(Y_{2}-2\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\right)-5\dot{z}e^{-z}\left(2\alpha m_{0}z+a_{0}\right)(Y_{2}+\partial_{y}Y_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{5\dot{\mu}e^{-z}}{2(1+\mu)}(2\alpha m_{0}z+a_{0})\Gamma(\Lambda Y)-\dot{z}e^{-z}(A_{1}+B_{2})\phi-\dot{z}e^{-z}\phi\partial_{y}B_{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3}

\displaystyle=b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{s}\phi+10(3m_{0}z+a_{2})\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}Y_{2}+\frac{\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}\phi}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma(\Lambda F)+R\partial_{s}\phi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{4}

\displaystyle=2\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}(E_{1}+F_{2})\phi-\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\phi\partial_{y}F_{2}+\frac{5(3m_{0}z+a_{2})}{2(1+\mu)}\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}\Gamma(\Lambda Y)+15m_{0}\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}Y_{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{10m_{0}b_{1}\dot{z}}{(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(1-(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}\right)\partial_{y}Y_{2}+10\alpha m_{0}\dot{z}e^{-z}Y_{2}-5(3m_{0}z+a_{2})\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\partial_{y}Y_{2}.

On the other hand, from the definition of
Q_{2}
in (
2.3
), we compute

-\partial_{y}^{2}Q_{2}+(1+\mu)Q_{2}-Q_{2}^{5}=0,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

It follows directly that

\partial_{y}\left(\partial_{y}^{2}V-V+V^{5}\right)=-\mu\partial_{y}Q_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{6}+\mathcal{H}_{7}.

(2.39)

Here, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{6}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}\left(\partial_{y}^{2}(R\phi+U)-(R\phi+U)+5S^{4}(R\phi+U)\right)+\partial_{y}H,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{7}

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}\left((S+R\phi+U)^{5}-S^{5}-5S^{4}(R\phi+U)\right)+\partial_{y}G-\partial_{y}H.

Step 2.
Estimate on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. We claim that

\mathcal{H}_{1}=\frac{5}{2}m_{0}b_{1}\dot{\mu}\left(Y_{2}-2\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\right)-10\alpha m_{0}\dot{z}ze^{-z}(Y_{2}+\partial_{y}Y_{2})+O_{\mathcal{S}}(1).

(2.40)

Indeed, we rewrite the term
\mathcal{H}_{2}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{2}m_{0}b_{1}\dot{\mu}\left(Y_{2}-2\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\right)-10\alpha m_{0}\dot{z}ze^{-z}(Y_{2}+\partial_{y}Y_{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{5}{2}m_{0}b_{1}\dot{\mu}((1+\mu)^{-\frac{5}{4}}-1)\left(Y_{2}-2\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\right)-5a_{0}\dot{z}e^{-z}(Y_{2}+\partial_{y}Y_{2}).

Combining the above identity with Definition
2.14
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we find

\displaystyle\|\dot{z}e^{-z}(Y_{2}+\partial_{y}Y_{2})\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|\dot{z}e^{-z}|\lesssim\frac{|\dot{z}|}{s^{2}\log s},

\displaystyle\|b_{1}\dot{\mu}((1+\mu)^{-\frac{5}{4}}-1)Y_{2}\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|b_{1}\mu\dot{\mu}|\lesssim\frac{|\dot{\mu}|}{s^{2}\log s},

\displaystyle\|b_{1}\dot{\mu}((1+\mu)^{-\frac{5}{4}}-1)\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|b_{1}\mu\dot{\mu}|\lesssim\frac{|\dot{\mu}|}{s^{2}\log s},

which directly completes the proof for the estimate (
2.40
).

Step 3.
Estimate on
\mathcal{H}_{2}.

We claim that

\mathcal{H}_{2}=-\dot{z}e^{-z}(A_{1}+B_{2})\phi-\dot{z}e^{-z}\phi\partial_{y}B_{2}+O_{\mathcal{S}}(1).

(2.41)

Indeed, using again Definition
2.14
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we find

\|\dot{\mu}e^{-z}\left(|z|+1\right)\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\|\lesssim|\dot{\mu}|e^{-z}(|z|+1)\lesssim\frac{|\dot{\mu}|}{s^{2}},

which directly completes the proof for the estimate (
2.41
).

Step 4.
Estimate on
\mathcal{H}_{3}
.
We claim that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3}

\displaystyle=b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{s}\phi+30m_{0}z\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}Y_{2}

(2.42)

\displaystyle+\frac{\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}\phi}{2(1+\mu)}\Gamma(\Lambda F)+R\partial_{s}\phi+O_{\mathcal{S}}(1).

Indeed, using a similar argument as above, we find

\|a_{2}\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}Y_{2}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim|\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}|\lesssim\frac{|\dot{b}_{1}|}{s\log s},

which directly completes the proof for the estimate (
2.42
).

Step 5.
Estimates on
\mathcal{H}_{4}
and
\mathcal{H}_{5}
.
We claim that

\mathcal{H}_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{5}=2\dot{b}_{1}b_{1}(E_{1}+F_{2})\phi-\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\phi\partial_{y}F_{2}+O_{\mathcal{S}}(1).

(2.43)

Indeed, using again Definition
2.14
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we find

\|\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}Y_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\dot{\mu}b_{1}^{2}(|z|+1)\Gamma(\Lambda Y)\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\frac{|\dot{z}|}{s^{2}\log^{2}s}+\frac{|\dot{\mu}|}{s^{2}\log s}.

Similarly, we also find

\displaystyle\|b_{1}\dot{z}\big(1-(1+\mu)^{\frac{1}{4}}\big)\partial_{y}Y_{2}\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|\dot{z}|}{s^{2}\log s},

\displaystyle\|\dot{z}e^{-z}Y_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|(|z|+1)\dot{z}b_{1}^{2}\partial_{y}Y_{2}\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|\dot{z}|}{s^{2}\log s}.

We see that the estimate (
2.43
) follows from the above estimates.

Step 6.
Estimate on
\mathcal{H}_{6}
. We claim that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{5c_{Q}}{4}b_{1}z^{2}e^{-z}\partial_{y}\left(e^{-(y-z)}Q^{4}(y-z)\right)-b_{1}^{2}(E_{1}+F_{2})\partial_{y}\phi

(2.44)

\displaystyle+b_{1}^{2}(2X_{1}-\Lambda X_{1})\phi-10b_{1}^{2}\partial_{y}(Q_{1}^{3}X_{1}^{2})-10\alpha m_{0}b_{1}\mu\partial_{y}Y_{2}
